<div class="thumbcaption">
<p>Religion in Malta (2019)<sup id="cite_ref-2019_Eurobarometer_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2019_Eurobarometer-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r981673959">.mw-parser-output .legend{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .legend-color{display:inline-block;min-width:1.25em;height:1.25em;line-height:1.25;margin:1px 0;text-align:center;border:1px solid black;background-color:transparent;color:black}.mw-parser-output .legend-text{}</style><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:Dodgerblue; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> (83%)</div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"/><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:Orchid; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox</a> (2%)</div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"/><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:red; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Protestantism" title="Protestantism">Protestantism</a> (2%)</div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"/><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:DeepSkyBlue; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160;Other Christians (3%)</div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"/><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:Green; color:white;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a> (2%)</div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"/><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:black; color:white;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Atheism" title="Atheism">Atheism</a> (1%)</div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"/><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:white; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Irreligion" title="Irreligion">Irreligion</a> (3%)</div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"/><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:yellow; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160;Other religions (3%)</div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"/><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:grey; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160;Undeclared (1%)</div>
</div>
</div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:306px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Poliptika_ta%27_San_Pawl,_Lluis_Borassa,_1400ijiet.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Poliptika_ta%27_San_Pawl%2C_Lluis_Borassa%2C_1400ijiet.png/304px-Poliptika_ta%27_San_Pawl%2C_Lluis_Borassa%2C_1400ijiet.png" decoding="async" width="304" height="346" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Poliptika_ta%27_San_Pawl%2C_Lluis_Borassa%2C_1400ijiet.png/456px-Poliptika_ta%27_San_Pawl%2C_Lluis_Borassa%2C_1400ijiet.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Poliptika_ta%27_San_Pawl%2C_Lluis_Borassa%2C_1400ijiet.png/607px-Poliptika_ta%27_San_Pawl%2C_Lluis_Borassa%2C_1400ijiet.png 2x" data-file-width="690" data-file-height="786" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Poliptika_ta%27_San_Pawl,_Lluis_Borassa,_1400ijiet.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <i>St. Paul Polyptych</i> dates to the early 15th century and is associated with the medieval Università and the <a href="/wiki/Mdina" title="Mdina">Mdina</a> cathedral. Featuring the style of the Catalan Gothic, it was probably made in the workshop of <a href="/wiki/Llu%C3%ADs_Borrass%C3%A0" title="Lluís Borrassà">Lluis Borassa</a> and is a testament to the strong <a href="/wiki/Pauline_Christianity" title="Pauline Christianity">Pauline tradition</a> present on the islands since the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-257" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-257">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-258" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-258">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>The predominant religion in Malta is <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholicism</a>. The second article of the <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Malta" title="Constitution of Malta">Constitution of Malta</a> establishes Catholicism as the <a href="/wiki/State_religion" title="State religion">state religion</a> and it is also reflected in various elements of <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Malta" title="Culture of Malta">Maltese culture</a>, although <a href="/wiki/Entrenched_clause" title="Entrenched clause">entrenched provisions</a> for the freedom of religion are made.<sup id="cite_ref-constitution_34-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-constitution-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There are more than 360 churches in Malta, Gozo, and Comino, or one church for every 1,000 residents. The parish church (Maltese: <i>"il-parroċċa"</i>, or <i>"il-knisja parrokkjali"</i>) is the architectural and geographic focal point of every Maltese town and village, and its main source of civic pride. This civic pride manifests itself in spectacular fashion during the local village <i>festas</i>, which mark the day of the patron saint of each parish with marching bands, religious processions, special <a href="/wiki/Holy_Mass" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Mass">Masses</a>, <a href="/wiki/Fireworks" title="Fireworks">fireworks</a> (especially <a href="/wiki/Petard" title="Petard">petards</a>) and other festivities.
</p><p>Malta is an <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_See" class="mw-redirect" title="Apostolic See">Apostolic See</a>; the <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_the_Apostles" title="Acts of the Apostles">Acts of the Apostles</a> tells of how <a href="/wiki/Paul_of_Tarsus" class="mw-redirect" title="Paul of Tarsus">St. Paul</a>, on his way from Jerusalem to Rome to face trial, was shipwrecked on the island of "Melite", which many Bible scholars identify with Malta, an episode dated around AD 60.<sup id="cite_ref-doi.gov.mt_259-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-doi.gov.mt-259">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup> As recorded in the Acts of the Apostles, St. Paul spent three months on the island on his way to Rome, curing the sick including the father of Publius, the "chief man of the island". Various traditions are associated with this account. The shipwreck is said to have occurred in the place today known as <a href="/wiki/St_Paul%27s_Bay" class="mw-redirect" title="St Paul&#39;s Bay">St Paul's Bay</a>. The Maltese saint, <a href="/wiki/Saint_Publius" title="Saint Publius">Saint Publius</a> is said to have been made Malta's first bishop and a grotto in <a href="/wiki/Rabat,_Malta" title="Rabat, Malta">Rabat</a>, now known as "St Paul's Grotto" (and in the vicinity of which evidence of Christian burials and rituals from the 3rd century AD has been found), is among the earliest known places of Christian worship on the island.
</p><p>Further evidence of Christian practices and beliefs during the period of Roman persecution appears in <a href="/wiki/Catacombs" title="Catacombs">catacombs</a> that lie beneath various sites around Malta, including <a href="/wiki/St._Paul%27s_Catacombs" title="St. Paul&#39;s Catacombs">St. Paul's Catacombs</a> and St. Agatha's Catacombs in Rabat, just outside the walls of <a href="/wiki/Mdina" title="Mdina">Mdina</a>. The latter, in particular, were frescoed between 1200 and 1480, although invading <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Turks</a> defaced many of them in the 1550s. There are also a number of cave churches, including the grotto at <a href="/wiki/Sanctuary_of_Our_Lady_of_Mellie%C4%A7a" title="Sanctuary of Our Lady of Mellieħa">Mellieħa</a>, which is a Shrine of the Nativity of Our Lady where, according to legend, <a href="/wiki/St._Luke" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Luke">St. Luke</a> painted a picture of the <a href="/wiki/The_Madonna" class="mw-redirect" title="The Madonna">Madonna</a>. It has been a place of pilgrimage since the medieval period.
</p><p>The Acts of the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Chalcedon" title="Council of Chalcedon">Council of Chalcedon</a> record that in 451 AD a certain Acacius was Bishop of Malta (<i>Melitenus Episcopus</i>). It is also known that in 501 AD, a certain Constantinus, <i>Episcopus Melitenensis</i>, was present at the <a href="/wiki/Second_Council_of_Constantinople" title="Second Council of Constantinople">Fifth Ecumenical Council</a>. In 588 AD, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_I" title="Pope Gregory I">Pope Gregory I</a> deposed Tucillus, <i>Miletinae civitatis episcopus</i> and the clergy and people of Malta elected his successor Trajan in 599 AD. The last recorded Bishop of Malta before the invasion of the islands was a Greek named Manas, who was subsequently incarcerated at <a href="/wiki/Palermo" title="Palermo">Palermo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-260" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-260">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Maltese historian <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Francesco_Abela" title="Giovanni Francesco Abela">Giovanni Francesco Abela</a> states that following their conversion to Christianity at the hand of <a href="/wiki/Saul_of_Tarsus" class="mw-redirect" title="Saul of Tarsus">St. Paul</a>, the Maltese retained their Christian religion, despite the <a href="/wiki/Fatimid" class="mw-redirect" title="Fatimid">Fatimid</a> invasion.<sup id="cite_ref-261" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-261">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup> Abela's writings describe Malta as a divinely ordained "bulwark of Christian, European civilization against the spread of Mediterranean Islam".<sup id="cite_ref-262" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-262">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup> The native Christian community that welcomed <a href="/wiki/Roger_I_of_Sicily" title="Roger I of Sicily">Roger I of Sicily</a><sup id="cite_ref-malticross_42-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-malticross-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> was further bolstered by immigration to Malta from Italy, in the 12th and 13th centuries.
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:ZTN_city_center.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/ZTN_city_center.jpg/220px-ZTN_city_center.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="152" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/ZTN_city_center.jpg/330px-ZTN_city_center.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/ZTN_city_center.jpg/440px-ZTN_city_center.jpg 2x" data-file-width="500" data-file-height="345" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:ZTN_city_center.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/%C5%BBejtun" title="Żejtun">Żejtun</a> city centre <a href="/wiki/Church_of_St_Catherine,_%C5%BBejtun" title="Church of St Catherine, Żejtun">Parish church</a></div></div></div>
<p>For centuries, the Church in Malta was subordinate to the <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_Palermo" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocese of Palermo">Diocese of Palermo</a>, except when it was under <a href="/wiki/Charles_of_Anjou" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles of Anjou">Charles of Anjou</a>, who appointed bishops for Malta, as did&#160;– on rare occasions&#160;– the Spanish and later, the Knights. Since 1808 all bishops of Malta have been Maltese. As a result of the <a href="/wiki/History_of_Malta#Norman_Kingdom_of_Sicily_rule" title="History of Malta">Norman</a> and Spanish periods, and the rule of the Knights, Malta became the devout Catholic nation that it is today. It is worth noting that the Office of the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Inquisition" title="Roman Inquisition">Inquisitor of Malta</a> had a very long tenure on the island following its establishment in 1530: the last Inquisitor departed from the Islands in 1798 after the Knights capitulated to the forces of <a href="/wiki/Napoleon_Bonaparte" class="mw-redirect" title="Napoleon Bonaparte">Napoleon Bonaparte</a>. During the period of the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Venice" title="Republic of Venice">Republic of Venice</a>, several Maltese families emigrated to <a href="/wiki/Corfu" title="Corfu">Corfu</a>. Their descendants account for about two-thirds of the community of some 4,000 Catholics that now live on that island.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_Saint_George_in_Valletta.jpeg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_Saint_George_in_Valletta.jpeg/220px-Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_Saint_George_in_Valletta.jpeg" decoding="async" width="220" height="288" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_Saint_George_in_Valletta.jpeg/330px-Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_Saint_George_in_Valletta.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_Saint_George_in_Valletta.jpeg/440px-Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_Saint_George_in_Valletta.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="2538" data-file-height="3328" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_Saint_George_in_Valletta.jpeg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/Church_of_St_George,_Valletta" title="Church of St George, Valletta">Greek Orthodox church of St. George</a> in Valletta</div></div></div>
<p>The patron saints of Malta are <a href="/wiki/Paul_the_Apostle" title="Paul the Apostle">Saint Paul</a>, <a href="/wiki/Saint_Publius" title="Saint Publius">Saint Publius</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Agatha_of_Sicily" title="Agatha of Sicily">Saint Agatha</a>. Although not a patron saint, <a href="/wiki/George_Preca" title="George Preca">St George Preca</a> (San Ġorġ Preca) is greatly revered as the second canonised Maltese saint after St. Publius. <a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XVI" title="Pope Benedict XVI">Pope Benedict XVI</a> canonised Preca on 3 June 2007. A number of Maltese individuals are recognised as <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">Blessed</a>, including <a href="/wiki/Maria_Adeodata_Pisani" title="Maria Adeodata Pisani">Maria Adeodata Pisani</a> and <a href="/wiki/Nazju_Falzon" title="Nazju Falzon">Nazju Falzon</a>, with <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a> having <a href="/wiki/Beatified" class="mw-redirect" title="Beatified">beatified</a> them in 2001.
</p><p>Various Catholic religious orders are present in Malta, including the <a href="/wiki/Jesuits" title="Jesuits">Jesuits</a>, <a href="/wiki/Franciscans" title="Franciscans">Franciscans</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Order" title="Dominican Order">Dominicans</a>, <a href="/wiki/Carmelites" title="Carmelites">Carmelites</a> and <a href="/wiki/Little_Sisters_of_the_Poor" title="Little Sisters of the Poor">Little Sisters of the Poor</a>.
</p><p>Most congregants of the local <a href="/wiki/Protestant" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant">Protestant</a> churches are not Maltese; their congregations draw on the many British retirees living in the country and vacationers from many other nations. There include <a href="/wiki/St._Andrew%27s_Scots_Church,_Malta" title="St. Andrew&#39;s Scots Church, Malta">St. Andrew's Scots Church</a> in Valletta (a joint <a href="/wiki/Presbyterian" class="mw-redirect" title="Presbyterian">Presbyterian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Methodist_Church_of_Great_Britain" title="Methodist Church of Great Britain">Methodist</a> congregation) and <a href="/wiki/St_Paul%27s_Anglican_Cathedral" class="mw-redirect" title="St Paul&#39;s Anglican Cathedral">St Paul's Anglican Cathedral</a>. There are several Charismatic, Pentecostal, and Baptist churches, including the Bible Baptist Church, Knisja Evanġelika Battista, and <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.tecmalta.org/">Trinity Evangelical Church</a> – a Reformed Baptist Church. The members of these churches are mainly Maltese.
</p><p>There are also a <a href="/wiki/Seventh-day_Adventist_Church" title="Seventh-day Adventist Church">Seventh-day Adventist</a> church in <a href="/wiki/Birkirkara" title="Birkirkara">Birkirkara</a>, and a <a href="/wiki/New_Apostolic_Church" title="New Apostolic Church">New Apostolic Church</a> congregation founded in 1983 in Gwardamangia.<sup id="cite_ref-archive.maltatoday.com.mt_263-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-archive.maltatoday.com.mt-263">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup> There are approximately 600 <a href="/wiki/Jehovah%27s_Witnesses" title="Jehovah&#39;s Witnesses">Jehovah's Witnesses</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-264" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-264">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints" title="The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints">The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints</a> (LDS Church) is also represented.
</p><p>The Jewish population of Malta reached its peak in the Middle Ages under Norman rule. In 1479, Malta and <a href="/wiki/Sicily" title="Sicily">Sicily</a> came under <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Aragon" title="Kingdom of Aragon">Aragonese</a> rule and the <a href="/wiki/Alhambra_Decree" title="Alhambra Decree">Alhambra Decree</a> of 1492 forced all Jews to leave the country, permitting them to take with them only a few of their belongings. Several dozen Maltese Jews may have <a href="/wiki/Religious_conversion" title="Religious conversion">converted</a> to Christianity at the time to remain in the country. Today, there are two Jewish congregations.<sup id="cite_ref-archive.maltatoday.com.mt_263-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-archive.maltatoday.com.mt-263">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup>In 2019 the Jewish community in Malta gathered around 150 persons, slightly more than the 120 (of which 80 were active) estimated in 2003, and mostly elderly. Many among the newer generations decided to settle abroad, including in England and Israel. Most contemporary Maltese Jews are Sephardi, however, an Ashkenazi prayer book is used. In 2013 the Chabad Jewish Center in Malta was founded by Rabbi Haim Shalom Segal and his wife, Haya Moshka Segal.
</p><p>The Maltese Jews found themselves without a synagogue when the building on Spur Street was demolished in 1979. In 1984, a new synagogue was opened at 182 Strada San Orsola, but it had to close down in 1995 as the building was collapsing.[14] In 2000, a new synagogue was built in Ta' Xbiex with donations from the United States and the UK. The Jewish Foundation of Malta now manages it along with a Jewish Center.
</p><p>There is one Muslim mosque, the <a href="/wiki/Mariam_Al-Batool_Mosque" title="Mariam Al-Batool Mosque">Mariam Al-Batool Mosque</a>. A Muslim primary school recently opened. Of the estimated 3,000 <a href="/wiki/Islam_in_Malta" title="Islam in Malta">Muslims in Malta</a>, approximately 2,250 are foreigners, approximately 600 are naturalised citizens, and approximately 150 are native-born Maltese.<sup id="cite_ref-265" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-265">&#91;265&#93;</a></sup>
<a href="/wiki/Zen_Buddhism" class="mw-redirect" title="Zen Buddhism">Zen Buddhism</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith" title="Baháʼí Faith">Baháʼí Faith</a> claim some 40 members.<sup id="cite_ref-archive.maltatoday.com.mt_263-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-archive.maltatoday.com.mt-263">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In a survey held by the <a href="/wiki/Malta_Today" title="Malta Today">Malta Today</a>, the overwhelming majority of the Maltese population adheres to Christianity (95.2%) with Catholicism as the main denomination (93.9%).
According to the same report, 4.5% of the population declared themselves as either atheist or agnostic, one of the lowest figures in Europe.<sup id="cite_ref-2018_survey_266-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2018_survey-266">&#91;266&#93;</a></sup> According to a <a href="/wiki/Eurobarometer" title="Eurobarometer">Eurobarometer</a> survey conducted in 2019, 83% of the population identified as <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-2019_Eurobarometer_4-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2019_Eurobarometer-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> The number of <a href="/wiki/Atheists" class="mw-redirect" title="Atheists">atheists</a> has doubled from 2014 to 2018. <a href="/wiki/Non-religious" class="mw-redirect" title="Non-religious">Non-religious</a> people have a higher risk of suffering from discrimination, such as lack of trust by society and unequal treatment by institutions. In the 2015 edition of the annual <a href="/wiki/International_Humanist_and_Ethical_Union#The_Freedom_of_Thought_Report" class="mw-redirect" title="International Humanist and Ethical Union">Freedom of Thought Report</a> from the <a href="/wiki/International_Humanist_and_Ethical_Union" class="mw-redirect" title="International Humanist and Ethical Union">International Humanist and Ethical Union</a>, Malta was in the category of "severe discrimination". In 2016, following the <a href="/wiki/Blasphemy_law#Malta" title="Blasphemy law">abolishment of blasphemy law</a>, Malta was shifted to the category of "systematic discrimination" (which is the same category as most EU countries).<sup id="cite_ref-267" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-267">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Migration">Migration</span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Inbound_migration">Inbound migration</span></h4>
<table class="toc" cellpadding="0" cellspacing="0" style="width:25%; float:right; text-align:center; clear:all; margin-left:8px; font-size:90%;">
